\operatorname{\mu_{\mathsf{C}Z}}({{{\bf\it x}}})\geq 2
for all periodic
Reeb orbits for the chosen contact form for the link and so

\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k}_{+}(\mathsf{F}_{X,x})=0
for
k\geq 2
. Now invoke the long exact sequence (
5.7
) to deduce that

\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k}(\mathsf{F}_{X,x})=0
for
k=2,k\geq 4
,
\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{3}(\mathsf{F}_{X,x})\simeq H^{3}({\mathsf{F}_{X,x}})
, where we
use that the Milnor fiber of a three-dimensional
IHS
having the homotopy type of a
3
-sphere,
only has cohomology in ranks
0
and
3
.
∎

Chapter 6
Matrix factorizations and Hochschild cohomology

Introduction

Since direct calculation of symplectic cohomology is often not possible, in recent years a roundabout way has been proposed which
gives a route to calculate symplectic cohomology for the Milnor fiber of several classes of invertible matrix singularities.
In outline this goes as follows.

(1)

One first applies the classical theory of matrix factorizations. One can assign
many matrix factorizations to a given
IHS
, but there is one
that plays a predominant role, its Koszul matrix factorization. All matrix factorizations come with their cohomology. It should be considered as a first rough invariant. of the
IHS
.

(2)

The next step is to reinterpret this invariant via Hochschild cohomology. This is quite intricate and uses the entire category of matrix factorizations of a given
IHS
.
Indeed, the supplementary structure of dg-category
allows to apply the machinery of categorical Hochschild cohomology.

(3)

For invertible matrix singularities one refines Koszul cohomology in such a way that the extra symmetry of these singularities is reflected therein.
This step uses equivariant matrix factorizations. For many classes of invertible matrix singularities the resulting refined Hochschild cohomology has been calculated.

(4)

The categorical framework allows comparison with other categories associated to invertible matrix singularities which are rich enough so
that Hochschild cohomology makes sense for them. These categories are relevant because their Hochschild cohomology is precisely the symplectic cohomology
of the Milnor fiber of that singularity.
1
1

1

Rather, of the ”dual” singularity associated to the transpose matrix.

The associated homotopy categories are conjecturally equivalent to
the homotopy categories of equivariant matrix factorizations (for any given invertible matrix singularity, or its ”dual”).
Since these conjectures have been shown to hold under easily verifiable conditions,
the refined Hochschild cohomology in these cases – for which there is an explicit formula – is thus the same as the symplectic cohomology.

In this, admittedly long chapter only steps (a)–(c) will be dealt with. Sections
6.1
and
6.2
treat step (a).
This basically reports on D. Eisenbud’s original treatment
[
Eis80
]
of matrix factorizations. The categorical reformulation of step (b) is due to T. Dyckerhoff in
[
Dyc11
]
.
The main ideas from loc. cit. will be explained in Section
6.4
after the preliminary Section
6.3
.

The chapter ends with a brief summary the calculation of Hochschild cohomology in the equivariant setting, with the necessary details in the
case of invertible matrix singularities. This will be further detailed in Chapter
8
.

Step (d) will be briefly explained in a later chapter; see in particular Section
8.2
.

Up to now
IHS
s have been defined by polynomials
w\in{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
vanishing in the origin, but
only the germ of
w
at the origin is of importance. The maximal ideal
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
corresponding to
the origin thus plays a special role. In the algebraic setting of a polynomial ring

R=k[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
over any algebraically closed field
k
the
maximal ideal
{{\mathfrak{m}}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
is called the irrelevant ideal.

S=R/w
is the ring of functions on the
IHS
given by
\{w=0\}
.

Much of what follows can be rephrased in terms of
local rings
(R,{{\mathfrak{m}}})
, where
w\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}
. For instance, the
{{\mathfrak{m}}}
-adic completion of
R=k[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
is the ring

\widehat{R}:=k[\![x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]\!]
of formal power series in
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}
.
It is a regular local ring in which the irrelevant ideal now becomes the maximal ideal.
If
k={\mathbb{C}}
, and using the usual (classical) topology), one can work in the local ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{0}}
, of germs at
\mathbf{0}

of holomorphic functions on
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
in which the irrelevant ideal is the maximal ideal and
w=0
then defines an
IHS
.
The ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbf{0}}
is not only local, it is the subring of
{\mathbb{C}}[\![x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]\!]
consisting of
convergent power series.

6.1.
Basics on matrix factorizations

6.1.A.
Matrix factorizations are related to singularities

Consider an
IHS
defined by a polynomial
w\in R=k[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
with an isolated singularity at the origin.
Then
0\to R\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\cdot w\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}R\to S:=R/(f)
is a minimal free
R
-resolution of
S
as an
R
-module. Setting
d_{1}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}_{R}
and

d_{0}=\text{\rm id}_{R}
, one has
d_{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{0}=d_{0}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{1}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
. This is an example of a matrix factorization of
w
:

Definition 6.1
.

Let
(R,{{\mathfrak{m}}})
be as above and let
w\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}
. Then

(1)

A
matrix factorization
of
w
is a free
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
R
-module
X
of finite rank equipped with an odd degree
R
-linear map
d:X\to X
such that
d\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
.
In other words,
X^{0}\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{0}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}X^{1}
and
X^{1}\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{1}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}X^{0}
with
d_{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{0}=d_{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{0}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
(as above).

(2)

A morphism
\phi:(X,d)\to(Y,d^{\prime})
of matrix factorizations is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded map
\phi

such that
\phi\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{\prime}=d\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\phi
.

Choosing a basis of the free
R
modules
X^{0}
and
X^{1}
makes clear where the terminology originates from:
a matrix factorization of
w\in R
is represented by a matrix of rank
2k
,
k=\rank X^{0}=\rank X^{1}
,

(A,B):=\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\
B&0\end{pmatrix}\quad A,B\in R^{k\times k},\,\,\text{ such that }AB=BA=w\,I_{k}.

Example 6.2
.

(1)

The
zero matrix factorization
:
X^{0}=X_{1}=R
,
d_{0}=d_{1}=0
, is the matrix factorization of
0\in R
.
Note that there is no matrix factorization of
1
since
1\not\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}
.

(2)

Consider the double point
xy=0
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. Setting
R={\mathbb{C}}[\![x,y]\!]
,
S=R/(xy)
, the double point admits a matrix-factorization

(x,y)=\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise The operation of ”adding a double point” to an
IHS
at
\mathbf{0}
given by a polynomial
w\in{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]

consists of replacing
w
by
w+xy\in R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m},x,y]
. If
(A,B)
gives a matrix factorization for
w
, then

\left(\begin{pmatrix}xI_{k}&A\\
B&-yI_{k}\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}yI_{k}&A\\
B&-xI_{k}\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&xI_{k}&A\\
0&0&B&-yI_{k}\\
yI_{k}&A&0&0\\
B&-xI_{k}&0&0\end{pmatrix}\in R^{2k,2k}

gives a matrix factorization of
w+xy
.

6.1.B.
A geometric incarnation of matrix factorizations

Ultimately one wants to apply this to
IHS
’s, geometric objects, and indeed, a geometric flavor can be given to the above construction, if instead of free
R
-modules of finite rank,
one considers vector bundles over affine
n
-space
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
, or, more precisely,
locally free
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{n}}
-modules. So one replaces
R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]
with the sheaf of regular functions on
the space
\mathsf{Spec}(R)={\mathbb{A}}^{n}
.

A matrix factorization for a degree
d
polynomial
w
is a pair
({\mathcal{E}}^{\bullet},d)
consisting of a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded vector bundle

{\mathcal{E}}^{\bullet}={\mathcal{E}}^{0}\oplus{\mathcal{E}}^{1}
and two vector bundle morphisms
2
2

2

Degree
d
polynomials are sections of the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{n}}(d)
,
and as usual, for any vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
, one sets

{\mathcal{E}}(d):={\mathcal{E}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{A}}^{n}}(d)
.

d_{0}:{\mathcal{E}}^{0}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{1}(d)
and
d_{1}:{\mathcal{E}}^{1}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{0}
such that
d_{0}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{1}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}

and
d_{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{0}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}\otimes 1
.
Clearly, the preceding incarnation is the special case where the vector bundles are direct sums of line bundles (these are all isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{A}}^{n}}(d)
)
since morphisms between such vector bundles are given by matrices of polynomials.

More generally, replacing in he above definition
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
with any variety (or a scheme)
X
and
w

by a section
w
of a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
on
X
, one obtains the definition of a matrix factorization of
w
.

6.1.C.
Relation with maximal Cohen–Macaulay modules

First recall two basic concepts for finitely generated modules
M
over a regular local ring
(R,{{\mathfrak{m}}})
.
A sequence
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
,
x_{i}\in R
,
i=1,\dots,k
,
is called
M
-regular if
x_{i}
is a nonzero divisor in
M/(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1})M
for all
i=1,\dots,k
.
The projective dimension (abbreviated below as ”pd”) and the depth of
M
are then defined as follows:

\displaystyle\text{pd}_{R}(M)

\displaystyle=\text{ length of a minimal $R$-free resolution of }M,

\displaystyle\text{depth}(M)

\displaystyle=\text{ maximal length of an $M$-regular sequence}.

These are related as follows:

Theorem

(Auslander–Buchsbaum
[
Eis95
, Thm. 19.9]
)
.

If
R
is a regular local ring, then
\text{\rm pd}_{R}(M)=\dim(R)-\text{\rm depth}(M)
.

Suppose that the
R
-module
M
is an
S=R/wR
-module for some
w\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}
such
that
\text{depth}(M)=\dim(S)=\dim(R)-1
, then the above theorem shows that
\text{\rm pd}_{R}(M)=1
.
The
S
-module
M
is then called a
maximal Cohen–Macaulay
S
-module
.
By assumption there is a minimal free
R
-resolution
0\to X^{0}\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{0}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}X^{1}\to M

and since
w\cdot M=0
one can construct
d_{1}:X^{1}\to X^{0}
such that
d_{0}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{1}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.02269pt\raise 11.93056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.93056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d_{0}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 73.0557pt\raise 4.17824pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 73.05554pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.02269pt\raise-11.93054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.93056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{d_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 49.05548pt\raise-4.17824pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 98.85893pt\raise-5.18748pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 116.61108pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 116.61108pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,\quad d_{0}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{1}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}.

Then from the injectivity of
d_{0}
one derives that also
d_{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{0}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
. It follows that
X^{0}
and
X^{1}
have the
same rank and so
d^{0}
and
d^{1}
are represented by square matrices of the same size.
So this shows:

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
(R,{{\mathfrak{m}}})
be a regular local ring,
w\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}
and
S=R/(w)
,
A maximal Cohen–Macaulay module
M
over
S
gives a canonical matrix factorization of
w
over
R
,

\textstyle{X^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}}
\textstyle{X^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}}
with
\mathop{\rm Coker}\nolimits d_{0}=M
.

Remark 6.4
.

Note that
M
, considered as an
S
-module receives a
2
-periodic free resolution

\cdots\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{k+1}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\overline{X^{k}}\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{k}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\overline{X^{k-1}}\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{k+1}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\cdots\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{1}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\overline{X^{0}}\to M\to 0,

where
\overline{X^{k}}=X^{0}\otimes_{R}S
for
k
even,
\overline{X^{k}}=X^{1}\otimes_{R}S
if
k
is odd,
and the
d_{k}
are induced by
d_{0}
and
d_{1}
. Note that this is a complex since
d_{0}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{1}=d_{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{0}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
is zero in
S
.

6.2.
Koszul matrix factorizations

The basic constructions leading to the relevant matrix factorizations
can be performed over an arbitrary commutative ring
R
with a unit
1
. The point of departure is a
free
R
-module of rank
k
with given basis
{{\bf\it e}}_{1},\dots,{{\bf\it e}}_{k}
and an
R
-module homomorphism

\phi:N\to R
. This defines
a
Koszul sequence
after
identifying
\phi
with the corresponding row-vector
{{{\bf\it f}}}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
:

N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it f}}):=\{0\to\Lambda^{k}N\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\delta_{{\bf\it f}}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\Lambda^{k-1}N\to\cdots\to\Lambda^{2}N\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\delta_{{\bf\it f}}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}N\},

where
\delta_{{\bf\it f}}
is the
R
-linear map given on
\Lambda^{j}N
by

(6.1)

\delta_{{\bf\it f}}(e_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{{\bf\it e}}_{i_{j}})=\sum_{m=1}^{j}f_{i_{j}}(-1)^{m-1}{{\bf\it e}}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\widehat{e_{i_{m}}}\cdots\wedge{{\bf\it e}}_{i_{j}}.

The derivations actually do not depend on the choice of a basis for
N

and
\delta_{{\bf\it f}}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\delta_{{\bf\it f}}=0
. The indexing is chosen such that the complex starts at
degree
-k
and ends at degree
0
with
N
. Then the cohomology groups
H^{m}(N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it f}}))
vanish for
m>0
and
m<-k
.
If
{{\bf\it f}}=f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
is an
R
-regular sequence, only
H^{0}
survives and equals the quotient ring
R/(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
. In other words:

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose that
{{\bf\it f}}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
is an
R
-regular
sequence.
Then the Koszul sequence
N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it f}})
is a resolution of the
R
-module
R/(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
, that is,

H^{0}(N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it f}}))=R/(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
and
H^{i}(N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it f}}))=0
for
i\not=0
.

There is also a dual version, using a vector in
N
which one identifies with a column-vector
{{{\bf\it g}}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\in\oplus^{k}R
,

N^{\bullet}({{{\bf\it g}}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}):=\{\Lambda^{k}N\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{{\bf\it g}}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\leftarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\Lambda^{k-1}N\leftarrow\cdots\leftarrow\wedge^{2}N\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{{\bf\it g}}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\leftarrowfill\crcr}}\;}N\leftarrow 0\},

where one reverses the arrows and defines

d_{{\bf\it g}}(y)={{\bf\it g}}\wedge y,\quad y\in\wedge^{j}N,\quad j=0,\ldots,k-1.

Considering this as a cohomological complex starting at degree
-k
, there is only cohomology in degrees
-k,\dots,0
.
If
(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
is an
R
-regular sequence, then only
H^{0}(N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it g}}))=R/(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})
survives. Here one uses the isomorphisms

\Lambda^{k}N\simeq R
, and
\Lambda^{k-1}N\simeq N
.

Observe also that

(6.2)

\displaystyle d_{{\bf\it g}}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\delta_{{\bf\it f}}+\delta_{{\bf\it f}}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{{\bf\it g}}=({{\bf\it f}}\cdot{{{\bf\it g}}}^{{\mathsf{T}}})\cdot\text{\rm id}_{R}

which defines a homotopy operator.

Example 6.6
.

(1)

Let
R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
and let

N=R^{m}
.
Assume that
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
is an
R
-regular sequence. Then

H^{k}(N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it f}}))=0
if
k\not=0
and
H^{0}(N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it f}}))=R/(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})
.
In particular, if
f=0
has an
IHS
at
\mathbf{0}
, then the partial derivatives of
f
form an
R
-regular sequence and the
Jacobian ring reappears as the cohomology of the associated Koszul complex:

\mathsf{Jac}_{f}=H^{0}(N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it f}})),\,N=Re_{1}\oplus Re_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus Re_{m},\,\phi(e_{j})=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{j}}.

(2)

(See D. Eisenbud
[
Eis80
, Sect. 7]
)
Let there be given a commutative regular local ring
R
with an
R
-regular sequence
{{\bf\it f}}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})

and let
w
be an element in the ideal
I=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})
, say
w={{\bf\it f}}\cdot{{{\bf\it g}}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}
where
{{\bf\it g}}=(\cdots,g_{j},\cdots)
is row vector in
Re_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus Re_{m}
.
This free
R
-module receives a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-grading by declaring
|e_{j}|=1
,
j=1,\dots,m
,
|r|=0
for
r\in R
.
This induces a degree on wedge-products by reducing mod
2
. The
Koszul matrix factorization
of
w={{\bf\it f}}\cdot{{{\bf\it g}}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}

is defined as

(6.3)

\{{{\bf\it f}},{{\bf\it g}}\}:=\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\Lambda^{\bullet}N)^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.16716pt\raise 12.41666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{{\bf\it f}}+d_{{{\bf\it g}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 92.42986pt\raise 5.12746pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 92.43057pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\Lambda^{\bullet}N)^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.16716pt\raise-12.41666pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.44444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{{\bf\it f}}+d_{{{\bf\it g}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 68.43141pt\raise-5.12746pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 138.94212pt\raise-5.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{{\bf\it f}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 155.36115pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 155.36115pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{R/wR}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right).

It is indeed
a matrix factorization for
w
with
\mathop{\rm Coker}\nolimits{{{\bf\it f}}}=R/(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})
, since
(\delta_{{\bf\it f}}+d_{{{\bf\it g}}})(\delta_{{\bf\it f}}+d_{{{\bf\it g}}})=d_{{\bf\it g}}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\delta_{{\bf\it f}}+\delta_{{\bf\it f}}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{{\bf\it g}}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
by (
6.2
).

6.3.
Matrix factorizations form a dg-category

6.3.A.
Some basic constructions

Fix a commutative ring
R
with unit
1
. Recall that a complex
(X^{\bullet},d^{\bullet})
of
R
-modules is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
R
-module
equipped with a degree
1
derivation
d^{\bullet}:X^{\bullet}\to X^{\bullet+1}
,
A homogeneous element
x\in X^{\bullet}
by definition belongs to some graded piece
X^{p}
, and

p=\deg x
is called the degree of
x
.

Definition 6.7
.

The category
\underline{C}(R)
of
R
-complexes has for its objects complexes of
R
-modules.
A morphism
f:X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet}
in
\underline{C}(R)
is a degree-preserving
R
-linear map respecting the differentials.

Note that there are also maps
X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet+d}
between complexes of any degree
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. These give

\underline{C}(R)
an enriched structure, that of a dg-category to be defined below in §
6.3.B
.
Let me observe here only that
\underline{C}(R)
is a so-called tensor category, that is,
one has a tensor product of two complexes defined by

(X^{\bullet}\otimes Y^{\bullet})^{k}=\bigoplus_{a+b=k}X^{a}\otimes Y^{b},\quad d_{X^{\bullet}\otimes Yv}(x\otimes y)=d_{X}x\otimes y+(-1)^{a}x\otimes d_{Y}y,\,x\in X^{a},y\in Y^{b}.

The tensor product
f\otimes g:X^{\bullet}\otimes Y^{\bullet}\to(X^{\prime})^{\bullet}\otimes(Y^{\prime})^{\bullet}
is defined as

(6.4)

f\otimes g(x\otimes y)=(-1)^{pq}f(x)\otimes g(y),\quad\deg g=p,\,\deg y=q.

This sign-rule is known as the
Koszul sign convention
.

6.3.B.
Introducing dg-categories

A category
\underline{A}
is a
dg-category (over
R
)
if

(1)

For all objects
X,Y
of
\underline{A}
the set
\underline{A}(X,Y)
of morphisms from
X
to
Y
is a complex of
R
-modules, i.e.,
\underline{A}(X,Y)
is an object in
\underline{C}(R)
.
Moreover,
d(\text{\rm id}_{X})=0
, where
\text{\rm id}_{X}\in\underline{A}^{0}(X,X)
.

(2)

The composition
\underline{A}(Z,Y)\otimes_{R}\underline{A}(X,Y)\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\mu_{X,Y,Z}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\underline{A}(X,Z)

(tensor product as graded
R
-modules) is a morphism of complexes of

R
-modules, that is,
d(f\otimes g)
maps to
d(f\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}g)
. Moreover, the composition
is associative, i.e.,

\mu_{W,Y,Z}\,\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\,(\text{\rm id}_{A(Y,Z)}\otimes\mu_{W,X,Y})=\mu_{W,X,Z}\,\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\,(\mu_{X,Y,Z}\otimes\text{\rm id}_{A(W,Z)}).

The associated
homotopy category

[\underline{A}]
has the same objects as
\underline{A}
but the
morphisms are the homotopy classes of morphisms in
\underline{A}(X,Y)
.

Example 6.8
.

(1)

A differential graded
R
-algebra
A
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded associative
R
-algebra
A=\oplus_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}A^{j}

with
algebra
derivations
d
of degree
1
, that is,

d:A^{j}\to A^{j+1}
,
d\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d=0
and
d(ab)=da\cdot b+(-1)^{j}a\cdot db
for all
a\in A^{j},b\in A
(the
graded Leibniz rule
).
This can be made into a
dg
-category
{\underline{A}}
with one object,
A
, viewed as an
R
-module, whose morphism are the elements of the algebra
A
. Its structure as a differential graded algebra makes
{\underline{A}}
a dg-category.
Note that for this to be a dg-category, the Leibniz rule is not required.

(2)

Another basic example is obtained by transforming
\underline{C}(R)
into a dg-category:
The category
\underline{C}_{dg}(R)
has the same objects and morphisms as
\underline{C}(R)
.
The extra structure comes from observing that the direct sum

\bigoplus_{d\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{d}_{R}(X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet})
of
R
-homomorphisms
X^{\bullet}\to Y^{\bullet+d}

of all degrees receives a derivation from the derivations on
X^{\bullet}
and on
Y^{\bullet}
:

d(f)=d_{Y}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}f-(-1)^{n}f\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{X}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{d+1}(X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}),\quad\forall f\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{d}(X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet}).

In this way,
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{\bullet}(X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet})
becomes a complex, the
hom-complex
associated to
(X^{\bullet},Y^{\bullet})
.
Usually, in a
dg
-category one just focusses solely on their hom-complexes.

6.3.C.
The dg-category of matrix factorizations of
w

Recall that a matrix factorization of
w
is a free
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
R
-module
X
of finite rank equipped with an odd degree
R
-linear map
d:X\to X
such that
d\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
. These are the
objects of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
. If one allows arbitrary free
R
-modules, these are the objects of

\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}
.
As in Example
6.8
.(2) there are associated hom-complexes
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{\bullet}(X,Y)
.
Taking into account the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-grading, such complexes have
essentially two components,
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{0}(X,Y)
and
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{1}(X,Y)
and two differentials

\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{0}(X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{0}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{1}(X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{1}}

given by
(d^{0}f)^{m}=d^{Y}\,f^{m}-f^{m+1}\,d^{X}
and
(d^{1}g)^{m}=d^{Y}\,g^{m}+g^{m+1}\,d^{X}
. Now
X
and

Y
are not complexes, but note that
d^{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{0}f=0
, for instance,

\displaystyle(d^{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{0}f)^{0}=(d^{Y}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{Y}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}f^{0}-d^{Y}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}f^{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{X})+(d^{Y}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}f^{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{X}-f^{0}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{X}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{X})=0,

since
d^{X}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{X}=d^{Y}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{Y}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
. The resulting hom-complex being
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded is denoted
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(X,Y)
.
In the remainder of these notes, also the categorical notation
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}(X,Y)
is employed, instead of
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(X,Y)
. Note that in the homotopy category
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]
the complex
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(X,Y)

gets replaced by
[X,Y]=H^{0}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(X,Y)
.

Example 6.9

(The Koszul matrix factorization revisited)
.

Consider the Koszul matrix factorization
\{{{\bf\it f}},{{\bf\it g}}\}
of
w
given by
(
6.3
) which is associated to an
R
-regular sequence
{{\bf\it f}}

of length
m
and for which
w={{\bf\it f}}\cdot{{{\bf\it g}}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}
. Place it in the dg-category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}

which consists of one object
\Lambda^{\bullet}N
, where
N=\oplus_{j=1}^{m}Re_{j}
, and has

\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}(\Lambda^{\bullet}N,\Lambda^{\bullet}N)=\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits_{R}(\{{{\bf\it f}},{{\bf\it g}}\})
as its hom-complex .
Note that the latter
R
-algebra itself can also be considered as a (
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded) dg-category with one object
and with endomorphism algebra
\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits_{R}(\{{{\bf\it f}},{{\bf\it g}}\})
. As such it is full subcategory of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
.

6.3.D.
Tensors and matrix factorizations

The tensor product of two matrix factorizations is defined using signs as for complexes:

Definition 6.10

(
Tensor products of matrix factorizations
)
.

Let
(X,d^{X})
be a matrix factorization of
w
and
(Y,d^{Y})
a matrix factorization of
w^{\prime}
over
R
.
Put

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}Z^{0}=X^{0}\otimes_{R}Y^{0}\oplus X^{1}\otimes_{R}Y^{1},&\qquad\quad Z^{1}=X^{0}\otimes_{R}Y^{1}\oplus X^{1}\otimes_{R}Y^{0}\end{matrix}\hskip 70.0001pt

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}d_{0}^{Z}(x_{0}\otimes y_{0})=d_{0}^{X}(x_{0})\otimes y_{0}+x_{0}\otimes d^{Y}_{0}y_{0},&d_{0}^{Z}(x_{1}\otimes y_{1})=d_{1}^{X}(x_{1})\otimes y_{1}-x_{1}\otimes d^{Y}_{1}y_{1}\\
d_{1}^{Z}(x_{0}\otimes y_{1})=d_{0}^{X}(x_{0})\otimes y_{1}+x_{0}\otimes d^{Y}_{1}y_{1},&d_{1}^{Z}(x_{1}\otimes y_{0})=d_{1}^{X}(x_{1})\otimes y_{0}-x_{1}\otimes d^{Y}_{0}y_{0}.\end{matrix}

Then
(Z,d^{Z})=(X,d^{X})\otimes(Y,d^{Y})
is a matrix factorization of
w_{Z}=-w\otimes 1+1\otimes w^{\prime}
.
Indeed, the signs are such that
d_{0}^{Z}d_{1}^{Z}=d_{1}^{Z}d_{0}^{Z}=-w\otimes 1+1\otimes w^{\prime}
.

In particular, the tensor product does not preserve
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
.

Observe that the hom-construction
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(X,Y)
applies also in the situation where
X
gives a matrix factorization of
w\in R
and

Y
gives a matrix factorization of
w^{\prime}\in R
and then it is the hom-complex
of a matrix factorization of
-w+w^{\prime}
. In particular,
(Y^{0},Y^{1})=(R,0)
is a matrix factorization of
0

(with zero differentials) and then
X^{\bullet}:=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(X,Y)
is a matrix factorization of
-w
. Explicitly: for
g\in(X^{\bullet})^{1}
one has
dg(x)=g(dx)
and for
f\in(X^{\bullet})^{0}
one has
df=0
so that in view of the signs
d\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}dg(x)=-g(d\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d(x))=-wg(x)
.
In particular, if
w\not=0
, the category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
is not stable under duality.

6.4.
Matrix factorizations as stabilizions

6.4.A.
Koszul matrix factorization as a stabilization

As demonstrated in Lemma
6.3
, for a regular local ring
(R,{{\mathfrak{m}}})
and
w\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}
, a maximal
Cohen–Macaulay module
M
over
S=R/w
gives a matrix factorization of
w

whose cokernel equals
M
. The category of such
S
-modules can be “stabilized” if homomorphisms

g,g^{\prime}:M\to M^{\prime}
are declared to be identical if

g^{\prime}=t\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}g\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}t^{\prime}
, where
t\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(M,M)
and
t^{\prime}\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(M^{\prime},M^{\prime})
factor over some free
S
-module.
This procedure gives the

stable category

\underline{CM}^{\rm stab}(S)
of Cohen–Macaulay modules over
S
.
It turms out that the above cokernel assignment functor (on homotopy level)

\mathop{\rm Coker}\nolimits:[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]\to\underline{CM}^{\rm stab}(R/w),

is an equivalence of categories. By definition, given a maximal Cohen–Macaulay module
M
over
S
,
the homotopy class of a corresponding matrix factorization is called
the stabilization
M^{\rm stab}
of
M
.

This generalizes to the situation of Example
6.6
.(2):

Proposition 6.11

(
[
Dyc11
, Cor.2.7]
)
.

Let
(R,{{\mathfrak{m}}})
be a regular local ring,
I\subset{{\mathfrak{m}}}
an ideal generated by a regular sequence
{{\bf\it f}}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})
.
Suppose
w\in I
, set
L:=R/I
and write
w={{\bf\it f}}\cdot{{{\bf\it g}}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}
as before.

Put
S=R/w
(so that
L
is an
S
-module).
Then the stabilization
L^{\rm{stab}}
of
L
(as an
S
-module) is the Koszul matrix factorization
given in Example
6.6
.(2), i.e.

L^{\rm{stab}}=\{{{\bf\it f}},{{\bf\it g}}\}\in\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}

Example 6.12
.

If the
IHS
is given by a
weighted homogeneous
hypersurface

w(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=0
of degree
d
, where
x_{i}
has weight
d_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
,
the Euler formula gives
\sum_{j}x_{j}d_{j}w_{x_{j}}=d\cdot w
. Replacing
x_{j}
with
x^{\prime}_{j}=d_{j}/d
,
this shows that the Koszul matrix factorization

\{{{\bf\it x}},\nabla w\}
of
w
represents
k^{\rm stab}
as an
R/w
-module while the factorization
\{\nabla w,{{\bf\it x}}\}
represents
the stabilization of the Jacobian algebra
\mathsf{Jac}_{w}
as an
(R/w)
-algebra.

6.4.B.
Technical interlude

The constructions in this subsection, which use the concept of stabilization, will be used in an essential way in §
6.4.C

and §
6.4.D
. The main goal is to replace Hochschild
cohomology of the category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
by the (classical) Hochschild cohomology of the algebra

\widehat{M_{R,w}}
which will be introduced in Corollary
6.18
.
It requires passing to
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}
where Toën’s results
[
Toë06
]
in homotopy theory
can be used.
I won’t detail these techniques but only quote the results that Dyckerhoff obtains using these.

Lemma 6.13

(
[
Dyc11
, Lemma 4.2]
)
.

As above, let
(R,{{\mathfrak{m}}})
be a local ring,
I\subset R
an ideal generated by a regular sequence and
w\in I
.

Set
S=R/w
and let
L
be an
S
-module whose stabilization
L^{\rm stab}

belongs to
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}
.
Let
X
be an object of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
, and let
A
be the set of morphisms from
X
to itself in
the category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}
considered as a ring under composition and let
A^{o}
be the opposite ring.

Then composition with
f\in A
gives
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}(X,L^{\rm stab})
as well as
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(X,L)

the structure of an
A^{o}
-module.
One has an isomorphism

[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}(X,L^{\rm stab})]\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\sim\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}[\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}(X,L)]

in the derived category of
A^{o}
-modules.

Now return to the situation of Proposition
6.17
. So
L=R/I
, and
I
is generated by
a regular sequence
{{\bf\it f}}=\{f_{1},\dots,f_{m}\}
, and
L
is to be considered as an
S=R/(w)
-module.
Recall that the Koszul resolution of
L
associated to
{{\bf\it f}}
reads as follows:

0\to\Lambda^{k}N\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\delta_{{\bf\it f}}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\Lambda^{k-1}N\to\cdots\to\Lambda^{2}N\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\delta_{{\bf\it f}}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}N\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\phi\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}L=R/I\to 0,

where
\delta_{{\bf\it f}}
is defined by Eqn.
6.1
and
\phi
is the natural map onto
\mathop{\rm Coker}\nolimits\delta_{{\bf\it f}}
. Applying the preceding lemma, one finds:

Corollary 6.14

(
[
Dyc11
, Prop. 4.3]
)
.

For a free
R
-module
N
of rank
m
, let

h:\Lambda^{\bullet}N\to\Lambda^{0}N=R\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\phi\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}R/I=L

the composition of the projection and the map
\phi
resulting from the Koszul resolution of
L
.
Then for all objects
X
in
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
and
f\in\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(X,L^{\rm stab})
the map
f\mapsto h\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}f
establishes a quasi-isomorphism

\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}(X,L^{\rm stab})\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\rm{qiso}}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}_{R}(X,L),

where

L^{\rm stab}=(\Lambda^{\bullet}N,\delta_{{\bf\it f}}+d_{{{\bf\it g}}})

is the Koszul matrix factorisation of
w\in I
(cf. Eqn. (
6.3
)).

6.4.C.
Compact generators

In this section
R=k[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
but later, for technical reasons,
it will be replaced by its completion
\widetilde{R}=k[\![x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]\!]
. Since matrix-factorizations take place
in
R
, this is always possible.
The goal of this section is to find
R
-algebras which as dg-algebra are homotopically the same as
the two homotopy categories
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]
and
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}]
and that
w\in R
has an
IHS
at
the origin. Assuming that
w={{\bf\it g}}\cdot{{\bf\it x}}
,
{{\bf\it g}}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})
,
{{\bf\it x}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
, the two main players are

(6.5)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k^{\rm stab}=\{{{\bf\it g}},{{\bf\it x}}\}

(6.6)

\displaystyle M_{R,w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}(E,E).

The
R
-algebra
M_{R,w}
will serve as the building block for constructing the desired
R
-algebra.

The entire construction depends on a crucial feature of these categories, namely that they are
triangulated
.
See Appendix A.3.14 of
[
Eis95
]
for more details on this concept.
Subcategories of a triangulated category stable under shifts, triangles, isomorphisms and direct sums (coproducts)
are called
thick subcategories
. Certain objects in such categories play the role of generators
and in the present setting yield the desired
R
-algebras.
The required technical definitions are as follows.

Definition 6.15
.

An object
X
of a category
\underline{C}
admitting arbitrary direct sums is said to be
compact
if

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(X,-)
commutes with coproducts, i.e.,

\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(X,\coprod_{j\in J}Y_{j})\simeq\coprod_{j\in J}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(X,Y_{j})

for all objects
Y_{j}
of
\underline{C}
,
j\in J
, and

X
is said to be a
compact generator of
\underline{C}
if

X
is compact and if the smallest thick subcategory of
\underline{C}
containing
X
is the entire category
\underline{C}
.

Compact generators in this sense can only exist within the category
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}]
and not in

[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]
since the latter does not admit infinite direct sums.
The following principle describes the functorial role of a compact generator.

Theorem 6.16

(
[
Dyc11
, Thm. 5.1]
)
.

Let
\underline{C}
be a triangulated 2-periodic dg-category admitting arbitrary direct sums and
admitting a compact generator
X
.
Let
[X^{o}\text{-mod}]
be the localization (as dg-modules) of the category of
X^{o}
-modules (in the set of equivalences).
Then the functor

(6.7)

\displaystyle\underline{F}_{X}:\underline{C}\to\widehat{\underline{C}}:=[X^{o}\text{\rm-mod}],\quad Y\mapsto\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\underline{C}}(X,Y)

induces an isomorphism in the homotopy category of 2-periodic dg-categories.

Consider
B:=(X,\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits X)
as a dg-category. Then
\underline{F}_{X}
sends
the homotopy class of
B
to
\widehat{B}
, that is,
B
considered as a
B^{o}
-module.

The concept of
X^{o}
-module requires some explanation. Normally the action of
X
on and
X
-module is from the left. To indicate that the action
is from the right, one speaks of
X^{o}
-modules, indicating that the action of
X
is ”reversed”.

Compact generators do exits for the category
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}]

due to Dyckerhoff:

Proposition 6.17

(
[
Dyc11
, Thm. 4.1 and Corollary 4.12]
)
.

In the present situation
E
(cf. (
6.5
)) is a compact generator
of the homotopy category
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}]
.

It follows that taking in Theorem
6.16
for
\underline{C}

the homotopy category of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}
, the object
E=k^{\rm stab}
is a compact generator.
So one can form the category
\widehat{\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}}
as in that theorem.
Note also that the objects of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
, being bounded complexes, are compact.
Recalling the formula (
6.6
), one then deduces from the theorem:

Corollary 6.18
.

The functor
\underline{F}_{E}
induces an equivalence of categories

[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}]\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\,\,\sim\,\,\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\widehat{M_{R,w}}.

In other words,
\widehat{M_{R,w}}
is a model for the derived category of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}^{\infty}
.

One can describe
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
in a similar fashion in case the ring
R
is a complete local ring.
In the situation of a polynomial
IHS

w
, one may assume this and then
[
Dyc11
, Thm. 5.7]
implies:

Corollary 6.19
.

If
w=0
determines an
IHS
(with singular point at
\mathbf{0}
)
the functor
\underline{F}_{E}
(defined by (
6.7
)) induces an equivalence of categories

[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\,\,\sim\,\,\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}\widehat{M_{R,w}},

i.e., in the derived category one can replace

\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
by
\widehat{M_{R,w}}
.

Concluding, the algebra
\widehat{M_{R,w}}
represents the derived category of the category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
, by
which the goal set at the beginning of this subsection now has been achieved.

6.4.D.
The diagonal construction

A further crucial ingredient for calculating Hochschild cohomology comes from
the diagonal construction explained in this subsection: see the proof of Theorem
6.25

In this construction tensor products of matrix factorizations for
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
and
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R^{\prime},w^{\prime}}
play a role
for the special case where
R^{\prime}=R=k[\![x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]\!]
, specifically, one uses

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}

\displaystyle=R\otimes_{k}R=k[\![y_{1},\dots,y_{m},z_{1},\dots,z_{m}]\!],\quad y_{j}=x_{j}\otimes 1,z_{j}=1\otimes x_{j}

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}

\displaystyle=-w\otimes 1+1\otimes w

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle=\widetilde{R}/I_{\Delta},\quad I_{\Delta}=(y_{1}-z_{1},\dots,y_{m}-z_{m})\quad\text{the "diagonal" of $R$ in $\widetilde{R}$.}

Since

\displaystyle w\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\text{ it follows that }\widetilde{w}\in I_{\Delta}.

If
X
is an object of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
, then
X^{*}\otimes X
is an object of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}
.
Recall further that to be able to speak of ”stabilization” in the category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
, one works over
S=R/(w)

and over
\widetilde{S}=\widetilde{R}/\widetilde{w}
in the category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}
.
The preceding considerations applies to this situation. Indeed, set

F:=\Delta^{\rm stab}\text{ as an $\widetilde{S}$-module}\text{ (an object of $\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}$)}.

Then using the notation of (
6.5
) and (
6.6
), Corollary
6.19
in the setting of matrix factorizations over
\widetilde{R}

gives an equivalence of derived categories

\underline{F}_{E^{*}\otimes_{k}E}:[\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}]\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;\sim\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}[M_{R,w}\otimes M_{R,w}^{o}\text{\rm-mod}],\quad Y\mapsto\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(E^{*}\otimes_{k}E,Y)

where one uses that
E^{*}\otimes_{k}E
is a compact generator of
\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}
.

Proposition 6.20
.

The functor
\underline{F}_{E^{*}\otimes_{k}E}
sends the stabilized diagonal

F=\Delta^{\rm stab}
to
M_{R,w}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}_{R}(E,E)
considered as an
M_{R,w}\otimes M_{R,w}^{o}
-module.

Proof.

The aim is to show that
\underline{F}_{E^{*}\otimes_{k}E}
sends
F
to
M_{R,w}
considered as an
M_{R,w}^{o}\otimes M_{R,w}
-module.
Apply Lemma
6.13
to
X=E^{*}\otimes_{k}E

and
L=F
which represents
\Delta
.
One finds quasi-isomorphisms (in the category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}
of matrix factorizations)

\displaystyle\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}(E^{*}\otimes_{k}E,F)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}_{R\otimes_{k}R}(E^{*}\otimes_{k}E,R)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}_{R}(E,E)=M_{R,w}.

Note that
M_{R,w}
under left and right composition is an
M_{R,w}\otimes M_{R,w}^{o}
-module:

(f,g)h=f\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}h\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}g
for all
f,g,h\in M_{R,w}
. Also
\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}(E^{*}\otimes_{k}E,F)
and
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}_{R\otimes_{k}R}(E^{*}\otimes_{k}E,R)
are
M_{R,w}\otimes M_{R,w}^{o}
-modules and one can check that the isomorphisms preserve this structure.
∎

Recall that
\widetilde{R}=R\otimes_{k}R=k[\![y_{1},\dots,y_{m},z_{1},\dots,z_{m}]\!]

and that the polynomial
w({{\bf\it x}})\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}\subset k[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
yields
w({{\bf\it y}})\in k[y_{1},\dots,y_{m}]
and hence

\widetilde{w}=-w\otimes 1+1\otimes w\in I_{\Delta}\subset\widetilde{R}
can be written as

\widetilde{w}=\sum\widetilde{w}_{j}(y_{j}-z_{j})=\widetilde{{\bf\it w}}\cdot({{\bf\it y}}-{{\bf\it z}})
.
Using this, the central result which will be used for calculating Hochschild cohomology is as follows:

Proposition 6.21
.

(1)

The stabilized diagonal
\Delta^{\rm stab}
, an object in
\underline{Mat\!f}_{\widetilde{R},\widetilde{w}}
, is represented by the Koszul matrix factorization
\{\widetilde{{\bf\it w}},{{\bf\it y}}-{{\bf\it z}}\}
.

(2)

One has
\widetilde{w}_{j}=w_{x_{j}}\mod I_{\Delta}
.

(3)

\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits(\Delta^{\rm stab})
– considered as a complex – is isomorphic to the Koszul complex for the sequence
\widetilde{w}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{w}_{m}

modulo the diagonal ideal
I_{\Delta}
, considered as a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded complex, that is, if

{{\bf\it w}}=(w_{x_{1}},\dots,w_{x_{m}})
, then
\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits(\Delta^{\rm stab})\simeq N^{\bullet}({{\bf\it w}})
(see Example
6.6
(1)).

(4)

H^{k}(\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits(\Delta^{\rm stab}))=0

for
k
odd and equal to
\mathsf{Jac}_{w}
if
k
is even.

Proof.

1) is clear and 2) is left as an exercise.

3) Apply Corollary
6.14
with
X=\widetilde{R}
,
L=R
and remark that
R
is an
\widetilde{R}/\widetilde{w}
-module whose stabilization is

\Delta^{\rm stab}
. Hence
\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits{\Delta^{\rm stab}}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}_{\widetilde{R}}(\Delta^{\rm stab},R)
. Note that
R
as
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded complex
has
R
in even degrees and
0
in odd degrees and so all derivatives are
0
. Moreover,
R
is considered as an
\widetilde{R}
-module
and so
{{\bf\it y}}-{{\bf\it z}}
maps to
0
under any morphism
\Lambda^{2j}N\to R
,
N
a free
R
-module of rank
m
. So in the complex
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{{\mathbb{Z}}/2}_{\widetilde{R}}(\Delta^{\rm stab},R)

only the derivatives from
\widetilde{{\bf\it w}}=\nabla w\mod I_{\Delta}
survive which gives the
Koszul complex for
\widetilde{w}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{w}_{m}
modulo the diagonal ideal
I_{\Delta}
, i.e. for the ideal generated by the partial derivatives of
w
.

4) Follows from the above by applying Lemma
6.5
. ∎

Remark 6.22
.

Using the diagonal construction one can define a product structure on
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
which under the equivalence of categories
M_{R,w}=\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}(E,E)\simeq[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]
corresponds to the
R
-algebra structure on
M_{R,w}
. So one might consider the homotopy category
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]
as a categorical incarnation
of the
R
-algebra
M_{R,w}
.

6.5.
Hochschild cohomology

In this section I shall introduce Hochschild cohomology, first for algebras, and then for categories.
The aim is to understand Hochschild cohomology for the category of matrix factorizations over a commutative ring
R
with a unit.
As before,
R
will be a polynomial algebra over a field
k
, or its completion.

6.5.A.
Hochschild cohomology for algebras

Let
A
be any associative algebra over
R
. So
A
is an
R
-module equipped with an
associative product. Now pass to the
R
-module

C^{d}(A)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{R}(A\otimes\cdots\otimes A\to A),\quad\text{($d$ factors)}.

By convention,
C^{0}(A)=A
.
The modules
C^{d}(A)
can be made into a cohomological complex with derivations

\delta^{d}:C^{d}(A)\to C^{d+1}(A)
given by

\displaystyle\delta^{d}f(a_{0},\dots,a_{d})

\displaystyle=a_{0}\cdot f(a_{1},\dots,a_{d})-

(6.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{d}(-1)^{i}f(a_{0},\dots,a_{i}a_{i+1},\dots,a_{d})+(-1)^{d+1}f(a_{0},\dots,a_{d})\cdot a_{d}.

Its cohomology is the
Hochschild cohomology
of the algebra
A
:

\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{d}(A)=H^{d}(C^{\bullet}(A),\delta^{\bullet}),

named after Hochschild’s article
[
Hoc45
]
.
As for ordinary cohomology, this group carries a graded cup-product structure coming from the product
on co-cycles
\gamma\in C^{n}(A
),
\gamma^{\prime}\in C^{m}(A)
given by

\gamma\cup\gamma^{\prime}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n+m})=(-1)^{nm}\gamma(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\gamma^{\prime}(a_{n+1},\dots,a_{n+m}),\quad\forall\,a_{1},\dots,a_{n+m}\in A.

The Hochschild cohomology can be also be described in terms of the
enveloping algebra

A^{e}:=A\otimes_{A}A^{o},\quad A^{o}=\text{opposite algebra of }A

as will be explained next. This is based on the observation
that the action of
A
on
A
by left multiplication makes
A
into an
A
-module while multiplication on the right
gives
A
the structure of an
A^{o}
-module.
There is indeed a complex of free
A^{e}
-modules that computes Hochschild cohomology, the so-called

bar-complex

C^{\rm bar}_{\bullet}(A)=A^{\otimes{\bullet+2}}
, a homological complex starting in degree
0

with
A\otimes A
and with derivations given by

d_{n}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n+1})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n+1}.

The modules
C^{\rm bar}_{n}(A)
are free
A^{e}
-modules under the operation
(a\otimes b)\cdot(a_{0}\otimes\cdots a_{n+1})=a\cdot a_{0}\otimes\cdots a_{n+1}\cdot b
since
C^{\rm bar}_{n}(A)\simeq A^{e}\otimes A^{\otimes n}\simeq\oplus_{j}(A^{e}\otimes 1)\otimes e_{j}
, where the
e_{j}
form a
k
-basis of
A^{\otimes n}
.
By definition, the associated cohomological complex is

C^{\bullet}(A,A)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A^{e}}(C^{\rm bar}_{\bullet}A,A).

There is an isomorphism of
k
-vector spaces
C^{n}(A)\to C^{n}(A,A)
given by

f\mapsto\{a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{m}\to a_{0}f(a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})a_{m}\}

whose inverse is
g\mapsto\{a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\mapsto g(1\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}\otimes 1)\}
,
as one easily verifies.
Hence

(6.9)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{d}(A)=H^{d}(C^{\bullet}A)=H^{d}(C^{\bullet}(A,A)).

The bar-resolution is a free resolution of
A
as an
A^{e}
-module
by extending it to the right by the multiplication map
A\otimes_{k}A\to A
. The Ext-groups
are thus the cohomology groups of the complex
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{A^{e}}(C^{\rm bar}_{\bullet}A,A)
.
Next, pass to the derived category
3
3

3

See for example Appendix A.3.14 in
[
Eis95
]
.

{{\mathsf{D}}}({\underline{A}}^{e})
which is built on complexes of
A
-bimodules such as the bar-complex, or its dual
C^{\bullet}(A,A)
.
In the derived language this gives
H^{d}(R\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{{{\mathsf{D}}}(A^{e})}(A,A))\simeq\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{{{\mathsf{D}}}(A^{e})}(A,A[d])
.
Summarizing, one has:

Proposition 6.23
.

\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{d}(A)\simeq\mathop{\rm Ext}\nolimits^{d}_{A^{e}}(A,A)
which in turn is isomorphic to

H^{d}(R\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{{{\mathsf{D}}}(A^{e})}(A,A))\simeq\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{{{\mathsf{D}}}(A^{e})}(A,A[d])
.

6.5.B.
Hochschild cohomology for dg-categories

B. Keller
[
Kel94
]
has introduced an analog of the bar-resolution for any dg-category
\underline{A}

which serves as a means to define
Hochschild cohomology of
\underline{A}
. In order to carry this out, the first task is to define tensor products
of dg-categories in order to define the analog of
A^{e}
.

•

The tensor product
\underline{A}\otimes\underline{B}
of dg-categories: its objects are pairs
(x,y)
of objects

x
of
\underline{A}
and
y
of
\underline{B}
and its morphisms are given by

\underline{A}\otimes\underline{B}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=\underline{A}(x,x^{\prime})\otimes\underline{B}(y,y^{\prime})
as dg-modules;

•

the dg-category
\underline{A}^{o}
, the one opposite to
\underline{A}
, has the same objects as
\underline{A}
but

\underline{A}^{o}(x,y)=\underline{A}(y,x)
.

•

the enveloping dg-category is

\underline{A}^{e}:=\underline{A}\otimes\underline{A}^{o}
.

One can attempt to define Hochschild cohomology by imitating what has been done for algebras:

\displaystyle\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{d}(\underline{A})=H^{d}(R\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{{{\mathsf{D}}}(\underline{A}^{e})}(\underline{A},\underline{A}))\simeq\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{{{\mathsf{D}}}(\underline{A}^{e})}(\underline{A},\underline{A}[d]).

The problem is then to find a substitute for the bar-complex which should represent

R\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{{{\mathsf{D}}}(\underline{A}^{e})}(\underline{A},\underline{A})
. Any such complex
is called a
Hochschild complex
.
There is indeed a categorical version
of the bar-complex as explained in
[
Kel94
]
but this complex usually is unsuitable for concrete calculations.
There exists a more suitable Hochschild complex via the diagonal construction, as now will be explained. But first, some more categorical constructions are
needed.

(1)

A dg-functor
\underline{F}:\underline{A}\to\underline{B}
between two dg-categories
\underline{A},\underline{B}
consists of a map
x\mapsto\underline{F}(x)
from objects in
\underline{A}

to objects in
\underline{B}
, and for any two objects
x,y
, a
k
-linear morphism
\underline{A}(x,y)\to\underline{B}(\underline{F}(x),\underline{F}(y))
preserving the identity
and satisfying the usual associativity condition.

(2)

Given a dg-category
\underline{A}
, a left
\underline{A}
-module consists of a functor
\underline{M}:\underline{A}\to\underline{C}_{\rm dg}(k)
. So for objects
x
of
\underline{A}
the image

\underline{M}(x)
is a
k
-complex and for any two objects
x,y
, there is a
k
-linear morphism
\underline{A}(x,y)\to\underline{C}_{\rm dg}(\underline{M}(x),\underline{M}(y))=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(\underline{M}(x),\underline{M}(y))
.
In other words, this gives morphisms of complexes
\underline{A}(x,y)\otimes\underline{M}(x)\to\underline{M}(y)

which describes the action of
\underline{A}
on
\underline{M}
.

(3)

An
\underline{A}
-bimodule

M
is a dg-functor
M:\underline{A}^{e}\to\underline{C}_{\rm dg}(k)
.

Examples 6.24
.

1.
The
identity
\underline{A}
-bimodule

or the
diagonal bimodule

\Delta_{\underline{A}}:\underline{A}^{e}\to\underline{C}_{\rm dg}(k)
of
\underline{A}
is defined by
\Delta_{\underline{A}}(x,y)=\underline{A}(x,y)
on objects
(x,y)
of
\underline{A}\otimes\underline{A}^{o}

and

\Delta_{\underline{A}}(\underline{A}(x,y)\otimes\underline{A}(y^{\prime},x^{\prime}))=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(\underline{A}(x,y),\underline{A}(y^{\prime},x^{\prime}))

on morphisms of
\underline{A}^{e}
. This is a left
\underline{A}^{e}
-module with action

(\underline{A}^{e}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\otimes\underline{A}(x,y)\to\underline{A}(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})
. In the case of an
R
-algebra considered as
category,
\Delta(A)
is the algebra
A
considered as an
A^{e}
-bimodule.

2.
A special case of
the identity functor.

Let
X
be a variety over the field
k
and let
\delta:X\hookrightarrow X\times X
be the diagonal embedding with image

\Delta
. Let
p,q:X\times X\to X
be the two projections. Note that
\delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}
and so for
a locally free sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}
on
X
one has
p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\otimes\delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}=q^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\otimes\delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
. It follows that there are canonical isomorphisms

q_{*}(p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\otimes_{O_{X\times X}}\delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\simeq q_{*}(q^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\otimes_{O_{X\times X}}\delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\mathcal{E}}\otimes_{O_{X}}(q_{*}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}\delta_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\simeq{\mathcal{E}}.

So the functor on the category of locally free
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-sheaves given by

{\mathcal{E}}\mapsto q_{*}(p^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\otimes_{O_{X\times X}}{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta})

represents the identity functor. In this sense
\Delta
”is” the identity functor on the category of locally free sheaves on
X
.
This functor can be extended to the dg category of complexes of locally free sheaves on
X
, or to the category of matrix factorizations
in the sense of §
6.1.B
.

The same construction for pairs
(X,Y)
of
k
-varieties,
{\mathcal{E}}
a coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module,
and with the structure sheaf of the diagonal replaced with any coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{X\times Y}
-sheaf
{\mathcal{K}}

defines the
Fourier-Mukai transform
of
{\mathcal{E}}
with kernel

{\mathcal{K}}
, a functor on the category of coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules to the category of coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}
-modules.

The diagonal also allows to define Hochschild cohomology for the scheme
X
as

(6.10)

\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{d}(X):=H^{d}(X\times X,R{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X\times X}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta},{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta})).

Equation (
6.10
) can be seen as an example of a general result due to
B. Toën
[
Toë06
, Cor. 8.1]
stating that the usual bar complex is homotopic to the endomorphism complex of the identity bimodule

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits_{\underline{A}^{e}}(\Delta_{\underline{A}})=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{\underline{A}^{e}}(\Delta_{\underline{A}},\Delta_{\underline{A}}),

and hence serves as a Hochschild complex. In particular, equation (
6.10
) shows that any complex representing

R{\mathcal{H}}om_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X\times X}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta},{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta})
is a Hochschild complex for
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
(identified with
{\mathcal{O}}_{\Delta}
).

By making use of the diagonal construction in §
6.4.D
the preceding observations
can be applied to the category
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
of matrix factorizations of an
IHS
at
{{\bf\it 0}}
given by a polynomial

w\in R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
. Indeed, by Proposition
6.20
the identity functor is represented by the stabilized diagonal.
Its endomorphism algebra as well as its cohomology has been calculated in Proposition
6.21
. The results thus reads

Theorem 6.25
.

The Koszul complex on
the derivatives
\displaystyle\{w_{x_{1}},\dots\ w_{x_{m}}\}
of
w\in R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
,
viewed as a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded complex serves as a Hochschild complex for
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
and hence

\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{d}(\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w})=\begin{cases}\mathsf{Jac}_{w}={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]/(w_{x_{1}},\dots\ w_{x_{m}})&\text{ if $d$ is even}\\
0&\text{ if $d$ is odd.}\end{cases}

Remark 6.26
.

As explained in Remark
6.22
, one can equip the homotopy category
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]

with an
R
-algebra structure through the quasi-isomorphism
\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}(E,E)\simeq\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}
.
The Hochschild complex respects the algebra structure and so this structure survives on the level of its cohomology.
Clearly, this is reflected in the above theorem:
the jacobian ring
\mathsf{Jac}_{w}
is an
R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
-algebra.

6.6.
The equivariant case

6.6.A.
Equivariant matrix factorizations

Assume that the
IHS
given by
\{\{w=0\},{{\bf\it 0}}\}
admits symmetries in the weak sense that

w\in{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
is a semi-invariant with respect to a group
\Gamma
of linear transformations of
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
, that is,
there is a character
\chi:\Gamma\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}
with

\gamma(w)=\chi(\gamma)w,\quad\forall\gamma\in\Gamma.

A
\Gamma
-character
\chi
can be viewed as a rank
1
free
R
module with obvious
\Gamma
-action,
and so, if
V
admits a
\Gamma
-action , also
V(\chi):=V\otimes_{R}\chi
admits a canonical
\Gamma
-action.

Definition 6.27
.

Let
(R,{{\mathfrak{m}}})
,
\Gamma
,
\chi
and
w\in{{\mathfrak{m}}}
as above.

(1)

A
\Gamma
-equivariant matrix factorization
of
w
consists of a pair

X^{0},X^{1}
of free
R
-modules of finite rank equipped with an action of
\Gamma
, together with equivariant
R
-linear morphisms

X^{0}\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{0}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}X^{1}(\chi)
and
X^{1}\mathop{\vbox{\halign{
#\cr${\scriptstyle\hfil\;\;d_{1}\;\;\hfil}$\crcr\kern 2.0pt\cr\rightarrowfill\crcr}}\;}X^{0}
such that
d_{1}\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d_{0}=w\cdot\text{\rm id}
(as above).

(2)

A morphism
f:(X,d)\to(Y,d^{\prime})
of matrix factorizations is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
\Gamma
-equivariant map
f

such that
f\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}d^{\prime}=d\raise 1.0pt\hbox{{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}f
.

The resulting dg-category is denoted
\underline{Mat\!f}_{w,\Gamma,\chi}
.
There is an important difference with the non-equivariant case: this category is not
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded.
This is caused by the action of the character. Instead, the
\Gamma
-equivariant hom-complexes are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{2k}_{\Gamma}(X,Y)&=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(X^{0},Y^{0}(\chi^{\otimes k}))^{\Gamma}\oplus\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(X^{1},Y^{1}(\chi^{\otimes k}))^{\Gamma},\\
\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits^{2k+1}_{\Gamma}(X,Y)&=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(X^{0},Y^{1}(\chi^{\otimes(k+1)}))^{\Gamma}\oplus\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(X^{1},Y^{0}(\chi^{\otimes k}))^{\Gamma},\end{split}

where the differentials are defined as in the non-graded case and where
M^{\Gamma}

stands for the submodule of
\Gamma
-invariants of
M
, i.e.
M^{\Gamma}=\{x\in M\mid\gamma(x)=x\text{ for all }\gamma\in\Gamma\}
.

Example 6.28
.

Assume that
\Gamma
acts on a commutative ring
R
and let
N
be a free
\Gamma
-module of finite rank.
Starting from
{{\bf\it f}}\in N^{\Gamma}
and a
\Gamma
-equivariant map
\phi:N\to R

such that
\phi({{\bf\it f}})\in R
is
\Gamma
-invariant, the two Koszul sequences given in Section
6.2
are obviously
\Gamma
-equivariant.
This yields a
\Gamma
-equivariant
matrix factorization for
w=\phi({{\bf\it f}})
. One can also find a
\Gamma
-equivariant Koszul matrix factorization
\{{{\bf\it g}},{{\bf\it x}}\}
for
{\mathbb{C}}

(with trivial
\Gamma
-action) using a
\Gamma
-invariant
IHS
given by
w=\sum g_{i}x_{i}
.
Below I’ll restrict myself to the following situation:

•

–

The ring
R
is the polynomial ring
R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
;

–

\Gamma
is an finite extension of
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
acting diagonally on
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
, i.e. there are characters

\hskip 27.0pt\chi_{i}:\Gamma\to{\mathbb{C}}^{*},\quad i=1,\dots,m\text{ with }\gamma(x_{i})=\chi_{i}(\gamma)x_{i}\text{ for all }\gamma\in\Gamma;

–

There is a character

\hskip-16.99998pt\chi:\Gamma\to{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\text{ such that }G:=\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits\chi\text{ is a finite group.}

•

–

The polynomial
w\in R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
belongs to the
\chi
-character subspace

R_{\chi}=\{w\in R\mid\gamma(w)=\chi(\gamma)w\},\gamma\in\Gamma

so that
w
is invariant under
G
,

–

w=0
has an
IHS
at
{\bf\it 0}
.

6.6.B.
Hochschild cohomology

In the situation of Example
6.28
, the techniques that have been used in Section
6.5.B
can be extended to the

\Gamma
-equivariant case. It is a special case of a more general situation studied at length in
[
BFK14
]
.

The calculation in loc. cit. is very technical, but the main ideas are already present in the non-equivariant setting. There are some
important special features of the equivariant setting to keep in mind:

1.

In the equivariant setting characters of the group
\Gamma
play a central role which, as
explain above, implies that the hom-complexes of the matrix factorizations are not periodical, but graded by the characters and so
are the Hochschild cohomology groups;

2.

as in the non-equivariant case, Jacobian rings
come up, but now involve only part of the variables associated to a character space.

The expression for equivariant Hochschild cohomology involves an extra variable
x_{0}
,
and so one introduces

•

V:={\mathbb{C}}x_{0}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{C}}x_{m}
;

•

The
\Gamma
-action extends to
V
(and hence to
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0},\dots,x_{m}]
) through the character

(6.12)

\chi_{0}:=\chi\otimes\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}\chi_{i}^{-1},\quad\gamma(x_{0})=\chi_{0}(\gamma)x_{0}\text{ for all }\gamma\in\Gamma;

Moreover, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
set

•

V^{\gamma}=\{v\in V\mid\gamma(v)=v\}=\bigoplus_{i\in I^{\gamma}}{\mathbb{C}}x_{i}
, where
I^{\gamma}
is the collection of integers
i
in
I:=\{0,\dots,n+1\}
for which
\gamma(x_{i})=x_{i}
.

•

w^{\gamma}
, the restriction of the polynomial
w
to the polynomial subalgebra of
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0},\dots,x_{m}]
spanned by the
\gamma
-invariant variables.

•

V_{\gamma}=\bigoplus_{j\in I-I^{\gamma}}{\mathbb{C}}x_{j}
, a
\Gamma
-invariant complement of
V^{\gamma}
in
V
.

Tensor-products and duals of
\Gamma
-modules are
\Gamma
-modules. In what follows, the dual of
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0},\dots,x_{m}]

is identified with the polynomial ring
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0}^{*},\dots,x_{m}^{*}]
where the
x_{j}^{*}
are given degree
-1
.
Hence
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0},\dots,x_{m},x_{0}^{*},\dots,x_{m}^{*}]
is a
\Gamma
-module as well.

In the present situation the following
\Gamma
-submodules of
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0},\dots,x_{m},x_{0}^{*},\dots,x_{m}^{*}]
play a role:

•

The Jacobian ring
\mathsf{Jac}_{w_{\gamma}}
.

•

The character space
\det(V_\gamma)=\Lambda^{\dim V_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}
with character
\kappa_{\gamma}:=\prod_{i\in I_{\gamma}}\chi_{i}
,

•

For any
\Gamma
-invariant subring
S
of
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0},\dots,x_{m},x_{0}^{*},\dots,x_{m}^{*}]
, the character space
S_{\chi}:=\{x\in S\mid\gamma(x)=\chi(x)\cdot x\}
.

Recall from Section
6.2
that the Koszul sequence associated to the regular sequence
{{\bf\it w}}=(w_{x_{1}},\dots,w_{x_{m}})

is denoted by
N({{\bf\it w}})
, and similarly for the regular sequence
{{\bf\it w}}^{\gamma}
associated to
w^{\gamma}
.
Using these conventions, the result from
[
BFK14
]
in this case reads (see
[
LU21
, Eqn. (5.2]
)

\displaystyle\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{t}(\underline{Mat\!f}_{w,\Gamma,\chi})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits\chi,\ l\geq 0\\
t-\#I^{\gamma}=2u\end{subarray}}\left(H^{-2l}(N({{\bf\it w}}^{\gamma})\otimes\kappa_{\gamma}\right)_{(u+\ell)\chi}\bigoplus

\displaystyle\hskip-90.00014pt\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits\chi,\ l\geq 0\\
t-\#I^{\gamma}=2u+1\end{subarray}}\left(H^{-2l-1}(N({{\bf\it w}}^{\gamma})\otimes\kappa_{\gamma}\right)_{(u+\ell+1)\chi}.

Since
w=0
(and hence also
w^{\gamma}=0
) is an isolated
IHS
at
\mathbf{0}
, Example
6.6
.1 tells that the cohomology of the Koszul sequence

{{\bf\it w}}
(and also for
{{\bf\it w}}^{\gamma}
) is concentrated in degree
0
and so only the terms with
\ell=0
contribute, i.e.,
the terms involving the
H^{0}(N({{\bf\it w}}^{\gamma}))=\mathsf{Jac}_{w^{\gamma}}
.
Unraveling the above formula then yields:

Proposition 6.29
.

Let
u\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, then

(1)

The contributions to
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{2u+\#I^{\gamma}}(\underline{Mat\!f}_{w,\Gamma,\chi})
consist of two types of monomials in the ring
{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0},\dots,x_{m},x_{0}^{*},\dots,x_{m}^{*}]
:

(a)

In case
\gamma(x_{0})\not=x_{0}
, every monomial in
S_{\chi^{\otimes u}}
, where
S=\mathsf{Jac}_{w^{\gamma}}\otimes\kappa^{*}_{\gamma}
;

(b)

If
\gamma(x_{0})=\ x_{0}
, every monomial in
(S\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0}])_{\chi^{\otimes u}}
.

(2)

The contributions to
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{2u+1+\#I^{\gamma}}(\underline{Mat\!f}_{w,\Gamma,\chi})

consist of every monomial of the form
x_{0}^{*}\otimes(S\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[x_{0}])_{\chi^{\otimes u}}
provided
\gamma(x_{0})=\ x_{0}
.

Chapter 7
Bigrading on symplectic cohomology as a contact-invariant

Introduction

A Gerstenhaber algebra, introduced in Section
7.1
, gives a bigrading on Hochschild
cohomology, as explained in (
7.2
). It turns out that a Gerstenhaber structure exists
on the symplectic cohomology of the symplectic
completion of a Liouville domain with
b_{1}=0
and with trivializable tangent bundle,
as is the case for the Milnor fiber of an
IHS
. Under favorable conditions, enumerated in
Theorem
7.6
, this structure is a
contact invariant of the link. It follows in particular (cf. Corollary
7.7
) that
for isolated cDV singularities
(X,x)
the resulting grading on each of the vector spaces
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{d}(X,x)
,
d<0
, is a contact invariant.

7.1.
Gerstenhaber algebras

A graded complex vector space
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{*}=\oplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{k}
,
is a
Gerstenhaber algebra
if it comes equipped with

•

a degree-preserving associative and graded commutative
product
\cdot
, that is,
a\cdot b=(-1)^{|a|\cdot|b|}b\cdot a
, where
|a|,|b|,\dots
, denotes the degree of
a,b,\dots
.

•

a Lie-algebra bracket
[-,-]
of degree
-1
:

–

[{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{k},{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{\ell}]\subset{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{k+\ell-1}
;

–

[a,b]=-(-1)^{(|a|-1)\cdot(|b|-1)}[b,a]
(i.e.
[-,-]
is graded anti-symmetric);

–

[a,[b,c]]=[[a,b],c]+(-1)^{(|a|-1)(|b|-1)}[b,[a,c]]
(the Jacobi identity).

•

the two products are compatible:
[a,b\cdot c]=[a,b]c+(-1)^{(|a|-1)|b|}b\cdot[a,c]
(the Poisson identity).

The subspace
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}
is a Lie-algebra over
{\mathbb{C}}
. Moreover,
since
[{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}\,,{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{k}]\subset{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{k}
, one has a degree preserving representation of
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}
on
{{\mathfrak{g}}}
.
Assume now that
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}
is a finite dimensional Lie algebra.
Then, from the usual theory of Lie-algebras (see e.g.
[
Hum72
, §15.3]
),
there is a Cartan subalgebra
{{\mathfrak{h}}}\subset{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}
, that is a nilpotent subalgebra which equals its own normalizer.
Cartan subalgebras are unique up to conjugacy. In case
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}
is semisimple, a Cartan subalgebra is the same as a maximal abelian
subalgebra.

Example 7.1
.

Consider the traceless matrices
{{\mathfrak{s}}}{{\mathfrak{l}}}(2)
of
2\times 2
-matrices. The
1
-dimensional vector space

{{\mathfrak{h}}}
of diagonal matrices is a Cartan subalgebra. The functional
\alpha:{{\mathfrak{h}}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
sending
H=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
to
2
gives an eigenvalue for the adjoint action of
H
on
{{\mathfrak{s}}}{{\mathfrak{l}}}(2)
with eigenvector

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
. The functional
\alpha
is called a root of
{{\mathfrak{s}}}{{\mathfrak{l}}}(2)
. The only other root is
-\alpha
.

Since
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}
cannot be assumed to be semisimple, the usual approach with root systems does not apply here.
It is still true that for a finite dimensional representation
\rho:{{\mathfrak{h}}}\to\mathop{\rm End}\nolimits V
the Jordan decompositions
for the non-zero elements
x\in{{\mathfrak{h}}}
are compatible and lead to a common generalized eigenspace decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{\lambda}V_{\lambda},\quad V_{\lambda}=\{v\in V\mid(\rho(x)-\lambda(x)\text{\rm id})^{n_{\lambda}}v=0,\,\text{ for all }x\in{{\mathfrak{h}}}\},

where
n_{\lambda}
is some positive integer. Moreover, the set of eigenvalues
\lambda(x)

for a fixed
\lambda
defines a linear function on
{{\mathfrak{h}}}
.

In the semisimple case the
V_{\lambda}
are genuine eigenspaces and are directly related to the roots. This
provides
V
with a multi-index grading as follows. Choose a basis
\{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r}\}
,
r=\dim{{\mathfrak{h}}}

for the roots. Then the eigenvalues occurring in a
{{\mathfrak{h}}}
-representation space
V
are integral linear combinations

\lambda=\sum\lambda_{i}\alpha_{i}
to which one associates
the multi-index
\vec{\lambda}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{r}
.
This gives the aimed for grading
V=\oplus V_{\vec{\lambda}}
.
In this situation, the adjoint representation of
{{\mathfrak{h}}}
on
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}
gives the so-called Cartan decomposition
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}={{\mathfrak{h}}}\oplus_{\alpha\not=0}{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1,\alpha},\quad\dim({{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1,\alpha})=1,\alpha\not=0
,
where now any non-zero
\alpha
is called a root.

In the general case the
(\lambda=0)
-eigenspace for the adjoint action of
{{\mathfrak{h}}}
on
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{1}
still equals the Cartan subalgebra.
However, instead of a root basis, one can only use a
{\mathbb{C}}
-basis and the multi-index becomes a complex multi-index.
In the applications below,
\dim{{\mathfrak{h}}}=1
and there is an honest finite complex grading on each of the
{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{k}
resulting in the bigrading

(7.1)

{{\mathfrak{g}}}=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ell\in{\mathbb{C}}}{{\mathfrak{g}}}^{k,\ell}.

This bigrading depends on the basis
\alpha
of
{{\mathfrak{h}}}
. Changing it by a complex multiple
\mu\cdot\alpha
replaces
the second grading
\ell
by
\mu\ell
. One calls it a
rescaled grading
.

Recall from §
6.5.A
that the Hochschild cohomology
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(A)
of an associative
algebra
A
is constructed from a complex
C^{\bullet}(A)
and if
Z^{k}(A)
is the sub-algebra
of the
k
-cocyles, there is a a Lie-algebra structure which comes from a
Lie bracket

Z^{k}(A)\times Z^{\ell}(A)\to Z^{k+\ell-1}(A)

generalizing the Lie bracket on
Z^{1}(A)
, the so-called

Gerstenhaber bracket
. See
[
Ger63
]
for the (involved) definition.
This bracket together with the cup product structure gives Hochschild cohomology

\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(A)
the structure of a Gerstenhaber algebra.

Example 7.2
.

Since Remark
6.26
states that the Hochschild cohomology
of the category
[\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w}]
of matrix factorizations for
w
can be computed from a Hochschild complex of
R
-algebras,
it has a natural structure of a Gerstenhaber algebra.

Observe that since
R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
, the
R
-algebras that occur here are graded and so
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w})
as well as

\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(\underline{Mat\!f}_{w,\Gamma,\chi})
receive an extra grading. This grading is an
integral
grading.

The above example motivates to consider
the abstract situation of a
graded
associative algebra,
A
, that is
A=\oplus_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}A^{\ell}
. As
in the example, the spaces
C^{k}(A)
then receive an extra grading. The one on

C^{1}(A)=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits(A,A)
is to be interpreted as an operator
E:A\to A

having integral eigenvalues
\ell
on the eigenspace
A^{\ell}
. Such an operator can be seen to be
a derivation on
A
and hence defines a class
[E]\in\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1}(A)
.
The operator
E
acts on
Z^{k}(A)
through the Gerstenhaber-bracket and preserves
B^{k}(A)
. Hence one gets
a second grading on Hochschild cohomology.
Usually one shifts the grading on
A
to achieve that the bigrading adds up to the degree of the
Hochschild cohomology by setting

(7.2)

Z^{k,\ell}(A):=\{c\in Z^{k}(A)\mid[E,c]=\ell c\}[-\ell],\quad\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k,\ell}(A)=Z^{k,\ell}(A)/B^{k,\ell}(A),

where
B^{k,\ell}(A)=Z^{k,\ell}(A)\cap B^{k}(A)
.
It follows from kernel of
[E,-]
on
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1}(A)
is exactly
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1,0}(A)
. If this space happens to be
1
-dimensional
it obviously is a Cartan subalgebra of
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1}(A)
and by unicity of such a subalgebra, one deduces:

Proposition 7.3
.

Suppose
\dim{\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1,0}(A)}=1
. The adjoint action of the Cartan subalgebra
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1,0}(A)\subset\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1}(A)

on Hochschild cohomology yields an integral bigrading (
7.2
) which satisfies

\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{m}(A)=\bigoplus_{k+\ell=m}\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k,\ell}(A).

Example 7.4
.

\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(\underline{Mat\!f}_{R,w})
as well as
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(\underline{Mat\!f}_{w,\Gamma,\chi})
come from Hochschild complexes of

R
-algebras with
R={\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{m}]
.

7.2.
How Gerstenhaber algebras lead to contact invariants

Recall that by Theorem
5.8
the symplectic cohomology of a Liouville domain
W
with
c_{1}(W)=0
has a graded product. More is true:

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
(W,\omega)
be a Liouville domain for which
TW
is trivializable. and such that

b_{1}(\widehat{W})=0
, where
\widehat{W}
is the symplectic completion of
W
.
Then
\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(W)
admits a Lie-algebra bracket of degree
-1
and, together with the graded product
\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(W)
, forms a Gerstenhaber algebra.

There is an excellent overview of the construction of the Lie bracket in
[
EL21
, §4.3]
.
The Gerstenhaber structure then provides a Cartan subalgebra
{{\mathfrak{h}}}\subset\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1}(W)
which is the generalized
0
-eigenspace

\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1,0}(W)
of the adjoint action of
{{\mathfrak{h}}}
on
\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1}(W)
. Assuming that

(7.3)

\dim{\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{1,0}(W)}=1,

the Gerstenhaber structure gives
\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(W)
a bigraded structure as in (
7.1
):

\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{*}(W)=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ell\in{\mathbb{C}}}\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k,\ell}(W).

It is worthwhile to observe that here one has
\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k,\ell}(W)\subset\mathsf{S\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k}(W)
, contrary to the bigrading
in Hochschild cohomology discussed in the previous subsection where
\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k,\ell}(A)\subset\mathsf{H\hskip-1.0ptH}^{k+\ell}(A)
